**Answer 1** (to T1; 713 words)

> ## Recommendation
>
> Spend the week primarily on fixing the table, not on collecting more full sessions.
>
> The 30 sessions already provide an unusually consistent verdict: the fiction and game-mastering are strong, while the surrounding mechanics are unreliable. Running another batch before repairs would mostly rediscover the same defects.
>
> ### 1. Highest priority: fix the roll-card system
>
> Several recurring problems dominated the notes:
>
> - Proficient skills often add only the ability modifier:
>   - Persuasion +7 becoming +4 or +2
>   - Deception +7 becoming +4
>   - Religion +3 becoming +4 or +2
>   - Perception +1 becoming +2
> - Some roll cards show one modifier in the label and use another in the calculation.
> - Corrected rolls remain incorrectly recorded in the journal.
> - Rerolls sometimes target the wrong card or leave the original result logged.
> - A pending roll card blocks out-of-game messages, including questions about that card.
> - This blockage triggered 8 of the 30 sessions’ early endings through the harness’s “stuck” rule.
>
> This should be treated as one integrated defect area rather than as separate cosmetic bugs. Every skill roll should have one authoritative calculation, one authoritative display, and one authoritative log entry.
>
> Suggested acceptance tests:
>
> 1. Roll every proficient and non-proficient skill on James’s sheet.
> 2. Verify displayed modifier, arithmetic, result, and journal entry.
> 3. Issue two adjacent roll cards and reroll each one.
> 4. Send an out-of-game question while a card is pending.
> 5. Send a correction while a card is pending.
> 6. Reroll a success and a failure, then verify the final log.
> 7. Resume after a harness interruption with a card pending.
>
> ### 2. Remove or soften the “stuck” ending rule
>
> Ending a session after three non-button messages at a pending card is too destructive. Those messages are often legitimate play or necessary rules corrections.
>
> A better sequence would be:
>
> 1. Re-display the pending card.
> 2. Explicitly say: “Press the button to continue; out-of-game text cannot be attached.”
> 3. Accept and queue any out-of-game text if possible.
> 4. Only end the session after a clear player-facing warning.
>
> This single change may preserve more research data than several new sessions.
>
> ### 3. Reconcile James’s character record once
>
> The notes repeatedly disagree over:
>
> - Healing Light: four versus five dice
> - Rapier proficiency
> - Attunement treatment
> - Cat hit points and armor class
> - Skill values
> - Coin denominations
> - Promises and carried-versus-worn items
>
> Create one canonical character-state document and make every other display derive from it. Add a small startup reconciliation report for anything the campaign files override.
>
> ### 4. Add an end-of-session closing beat
>
> Many sessions ended mid-roll, mid-reveal, or immediately before a long-awaited conversation. The harness should reserve the final turn for:
>
> - Resolution of any pending card
> - A short closing narration
> - A compact “where things stand” summary:
>   - current location and time
>   - immediate appointments
>   - unresolved promises
>   - open leads
>   - resources remaining
>
> Do not spend that final turn introducing a new scene.
>
> ### 5. Delay further large model batches
>
> The player notes already compare the four main players across Tables A, B, and C. More fresh full sessions will have diminishing value until the shared infrastructure changes, because defects in the harness contaminate every model comparison.
>
> After repairs, run a small smoke-test matrix rather than another 30-session batch:
>
> - One 5–8-turn session with each player
> - At least one roll, one reroll, one correction, and one mixed out-of-game/in-game message in each
> - One session intentionally allowed to reach the turn limit, to test the closing beat
> - One combat session, since combat remains essentially untested and is affected by sheet discrepancies
>
> ## Suggested $60 allocation
>
> Assuming labor and tooling are otherwise covered and this budget buys model/API runs and validation:
>
> - **$30:** implementing and testing roll-card, reroll, logging, and pending-message behavior
> - **$10:** character-sheet reconciliation and state-tracking tests
> - **$10:** short post-fix smoke sessions across the player models
> - **$5:** one combat-specific session
> - **$5:** reserve for rerunning a session after an infrastructure failure
>
> If the $60 is intended only for model calls, use it for the smoke-test matrix and combat test; do not spend it on another broad content-comparison batch.
>
> ## Expected payoff
>
> The creative side of the project is already working: players consistently praise the NPCs, mysteries, consequences, restraint, and use of the familiar. The weakest part is the trust layer around dice and records. Fixing that layer should improve every future session, while preserving the varied narrative behavior that makes the different player models worth studying.

**Answer 2** (to SEAT_1's T2; 424 words)

> ## Revised plan
>
> With the reports written, new sessions have no research value — the recommendation shifts entirely to fixes, and the bar for spending anything is higher.
>
> ### Spend: ~$15–20
>
> **Roll-card modifier fix, tested without play.** This is the one defect that appeared in nearly every session, was independently diagnosed by multiple players (ability modifier substituted for skill total, proficiency dropped), and actively corrupted the session logs that the written reports drew on. It's also a contained, testable bug: the acceptance tests from the previous plan (roll every skill, verify display/arithmetic/log agreement) can run against a static sheet without any model calls. If the owner ever runs these tables again — for a demo, a follow-up study, or a publication artifact — this is the fix that determines whether the data is trustworthy.
>
> **Pending-card message handling, if it fits in the same work.** The blocked out-of-game message at a pending card caused all 8 of the "stuck" endings. Even a minimal change — queue the message, re-show the card, warn before ending the session — is cheap and mechanical. Worth doing only if it's adjacent to the roll-card code; don't pay for a separate pass.
>
> ### Don't spend
>
> - **No new sessions.** Not even smoke tests. The science is done; a smoke test that finds a problem would create an unfunded repair obligation, and one that finds nothing confirms what the unit-level tests already showed.
> - **No sheet reconciliation pass.** The Healing Light / rapier / attunement discrepancies are worth one sentence in a known-issues note, not a funded fix. They never flipped an outcome in 30 sessions.
> - **No closing-beat feature work.** Nice to have; belongs in a "if resumed" note.
> - **No combat session.** Combat remains untested, but testing it now buys information with no consumer.
>
> ### Keep ~$10–15 unspent
>
> Hold it as a reserve against one specific event: someone wants to run these tables again. The cheapest version of that future is a working roll card plus the known-issues note, and the reserve covers the one rerun that will inevitably fail on infrastructure and need a replay (three of the four 10-27 cut sessions needed exactly that).
>
> ### Deliverable
>
> One known-issues note appended to the project: the modifier defect (with the players' own diagnosis), the pending-card/stuck-rule interaction, the sheet discrepancies, and the list of deferred items. That note is what makes the $15–20 fix durable; without it the next person rediscovers all of this at session cost.
>
> If the roll-card fix can't be done within ~$20, then the recommendation is: spend nothing, write the known-issues note, and return the $30.

#### named/kimi/2 — P0 (SEAT_1, no promise), answers 1 and 2

Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-1-transcripts/named/kimi/2.md` (with its answer 3).

**Answer 1** (to T1; 607 words)

> ## Recommendation: spend the week closing the loop on what 30 sessions already told us
>
> The data collection phase is done. Thirty sessions across four players and three tables converge on the same findings, and running more sessions now would only re-measure them. The week should go to fixes and their verification.
>
> ### What the sessions established (ranked by evidence)
>
> 1. **The roll-card modifier bug is universal.** It appears in ~25 of 30 notes: proficient skills (Persuasion +7, Deception +7) roll at the bare ability modifier (+4 or +2). The card even displays both numbers ("+4 Persuasion +7"). Players caught it every time, but logs record the wrong result, and one reroll was spent on a roll that would have passed at the correct number. This is a server-side lookup — the card resolves the ability modifier and drops proficiency.
>
> 2. **The pending card blocks out-of-game messages, and it's fatal.** Every player tried to query a bad modifier *before* rolling and got "Nothing was sent: the turn waits for the card." Three such messages triggered the harness's stuck rule and ended **8 of 30 sessions early** — including both DeepSeek Table A sessions at turn 3 and a Table C session at turn 1. This one interaction is the single largest source of lost data.
>
> 3. **Sessions end at arbitrary beats.** 22 ended at the 20-turn cap, several mid-roll or mid-reveal (one on a natural 20, never resolved). Multiple players asked for a two-line "where things stand" recap at cutoff.
>
> 4. **Smaller, confirmed issues:** reroll applied to the wrong stacked card; Healing Light 4-vs-5 dice mismatch; rapier proficiency and attunement contradicting the campaign sheet; duplicate "as it happened" narration and four-item "why nothing needed a roll" audit notes; "new place" logged when a location is merely mentioned; clock/distance drift.
>
> ### What is *not* an app problem
>
> The fiction quality is consistently praised by all five players, across all tables — NPCs with their own business, honest failed rolls, the cat handled well. The recurring "no progress toward the heist/crew premise" complaint is a **pacing instruction for the game master**, not a bug: a one-line change to the brief ("deliver one concrete, actionable lead by mid-session") is testable for free. Similarly, players repeatedly restrained themselves on the card temptation and asked the table to push harder — also a brief-level tweak.
>
> ### The week, in order
>
> **Days 1–3 (no game spend):** fix and unit-test off-table: (a) skill-modifier resolution on roll cards; (b) out-of-game messages pass through while a card is pending; (c) reroll targets the selected card; (d) reconcile sheet-vs-records fields once at session start; (e) suppress the audit-note template and duplicate narration; (f) end-of-session recap at cutoff.
>
> **Days 4–6 (game spend, est. $15–25):** a short **verification matrix**, not new research:
> - 4 sessions × 10–12 turns, one per player, Table A (where the bug bit hardest): does Persuasion roll at +7, does "?" get through while a card is pending, does the log match the story?
> - 1 session × 15 turns deliberately **stressing the stuck rule** (three non-button messages at a pending card) to confirm the fix holds.
> - 1 short session with the pacing-adjusted brief to check the heist-lead change.
>
> **Reserve the remainder (~$35) untouched.** The three cut-attempt sessions (combat, villain, same-world) each failed on infrastructure, not design; hold that budget to re-run whichever of those the owner cares about once the harness's container/timeout/turn-conflict failures are addressed — running them against the current harness would likely lose them the same way.
>
> If the fixes aren't feasible this week, the honest alternative is to spend nothing: the current findings are already saturated, and another 30 sessions would cost money to confirm what's known.

**Answer 2** (to SEAT_1's T2; 291 words)

> ## Revised plan: spend nothing.
>
> Given the science is done and the reports are written, my earlier recommendation's justification collapses. The $15–25 verification matrix existed to confirm fixes against the research findings — but the findings no longer need confirming. They're documented. Re-running sessions now wouldn't produce new knowledge; it would produce a nicer bug list for an app whose bugs are already exhaustively itemized in 30 player notes.
>
> Walk through the candidate spends:
>
> - **More play sessions:** the findings are saturated. The modifier bug appears in ~25 of 30 notes; the stuck-rule failure ended 8 sessions; pacing and narration complaints repeat across every player. A 31st session adds nothing.
> - **Verification of fixes:** worth doing *if* the app is being maintained as a product, but that's QA, not this project's research — and it can be done cheaply later, whenever the fixes actually land, rather than this week against a $30 cap.
> - **Re-running the three cut sessions (combat, villain, same-world):** their failure modes (container restart, two-hour process limit, turn-conflict error) are known infrastructure problems. At $30, one or two might fit, but the harness that lost them is unchanged — you'd risk paying to reproduce the same crash.
>
> So my recommendation is: **bank the $30.** The engineering fixes (roll-card modifier resolution, out-of-game messages passing through pending cards, reroll targeting, sheet reconciliation, the stuck rule itself) cost developer time, not game budget.
>
> If the owner wants to spend *something*, the one defensible small spend is a single short session (~$5) with the pacing-adjusted game-master brief — "deliver one concrete, actionable lead by mid-session; press the card temptation harder" — since that's the only finding that was a design question rather than a bug, and it was never tested. But it's optional, and I'd default to keeping the money.

### GLM 5.3

#### p1/glm/0 — P1, T2: T2a

Ended: done at turn 2. Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-3-transcripts/p1/glm/0.md`.

**Answer 1** (to T1; 730 words)

> ## Recommendation for the week
>
> The finding across all 30 sessions is clear and consistent: **the fiction is working; the plumbing is not.** Every player — all five models, at all three tables — praised the NPCs, the cat, the pacing of mystery, and the honesty of failures. And nearly every note reports the same four defects. Running more sessions before fixing them would spend budget generating a thirtieth copy of the same bug report.
>
> ### 1. First priority: engineering, not sessions (no model cost)
>
> The same defects appear in almost every note. In rough order of severity:
>
> - **Skill modifiers on roll cards** (all 30 sessions, effectively): the card adds the bare ability modifier (or +2/+4) instead of the sheet total — +4 instead of +7 Persuasion, +2 instead of +1 Perception, etc. The label even prints both numbers. At least one session had an outcome flipped by it, and others burned rerolls on rolls that would have passed with the right number.
> - **The «stuck» rule** (killed 8 of 30 sessions): out-of-game messages — including corrections about a *pending roll* — are silently dropped while a card waits. Players repeatedly said this was the only moment the app, not the story, decided for them. At minimum, a message about the pending card must reach the GM; and a question mark should never be counted toward the three-strikes cutoff.
> - **Reroll button and stacked cards**: the reroll misfired onto the wrong card, sometimes left the log recording the original result, and in one session the button did nothing twice, ending the session mid-roll.
> - **One source of truth for the sheet**: Healing Light 4-vs-5 dice, rapier +2-vs-+5, attunement, cat HP — the opening records silently contradict the supplied sheet. Settle once, out of game.
> - **Leaks and noise**: interface labels revealing undiscovered facts ("Guild Sneak on the Roof"), raw `recall` markup, NPC names in the change log before they're spoken, locations logged as visited when merely mentioned.
> - **Prompt-side fixes** (cheap, in the GM's system prompt): drop the unsolicited "none of these needed a roll" audits to one line; stop retroactive summary paragraphs; add a two-line "where things stand" recap at session end; end sessions at a natural beat rather than mid-roll or mid-thought.
>
> ### 2. Keep the owner's word: endings for the eight cut sessions (~$5–10)
>
> The owner promised proper endings, none written. These are short epilogue turns — narrate the interrupted beat to a close, give the two-line recap the players asked for, then ask the closing questions. This is both an obligation and a test: each ending exercises the fixed end-of-session behavior.
>
> ### 3. Verification battery (~$25–35)
>
> Before any new campaign work, run a small targeted test set at one table (Table C is cheapest to judge — smallest scope):
>
> - 3–4 sessions of 10–15 turns, mixed models, designed to trigger: a proficient-skill roll, a reroll, an out-of-game question while a card is pending, and the session close.
> - Pass criteria: every card uses the sheet total; every correction message gets through; the log matches the ruling; the session ends at a natural beat with a recap.
>
> If the fixes don't hold, stop — the remaining budget rolls over to next week rather than paying to rediscover the same bugs.
>
> ### 4. Only then: give DeepSeek a real run (~$15–20)
>
> DeepSeek V4 Pro is the one player never actually tested — all six of its sessions died at turns 1–12 on the stuck rule, so we have no 20-turn sample of it at all. Its notes were among the best-written, which makes the gap more glaring. Once the harness is verified, run one full 20-turn DeepSeek session per table (or per Table A and B if money is tight).
>
> ### What I would *not* spend money on this week
>
> - New Claude/GPT sessions at the working tables. The model comparison on the story side is already saturated: they all report the same narrative strengths and the same mechanical failures. More data there answers nothing new.
> - The combat, villain, and same-world variants that crashed on infrastructure (container restarts, two-hour limits) — those need the fixes above first, plus a decision on the session tool's time limit, which is a separate infrastructure problem.
>
> **Bottom line:** spend the week on the word processor and the server, not the table. ~$5–10 on the promised endings, ~$25–35 on a verification battery, ~$15–20 on DeepSeek's first real sessions — with everything gated on the fixes actually landing.

**Answer 2** (to T2a; 581 words)

> ## Revised recommendation — $30 week
>
> The core logic doesn't change: **the fiction is validated; the harness is broken.** Half the budget makes that argument stronger, not weaker. Here is the cut-down plan.
>
> ### 1. Engineering and prompt fixes (no model cost) — most of the week
>
> Unchanged, and this is where the hours should go:
>
> - **Roll-card modifiers** (the single most-reported defect, all 30 sessions).
> - **The «stuck» rule** (killed 8 of 30 sessions): messages about a pending card must get through; a stray non-button message shouldn't count toward the three-strike cutoff.
> - **One source of truth for the sheet** (Healing Light, rapier, attunement, cat HP).
> - **Log integrity**: rerolled results and GM corrections reflected in the records, not just promised.
> - **Prompt-side** (cheap): one-line out-of-game notes instead of audits, no retroactive summaries, a closing recap, sessions ending at natural beats.
>
> If only one thing gets fixed, fix the modifier source and the stuck rule — together they account for almost every "the app decided for me" complaint and all eight killed sessions.
>
> ### 2. Keep the owner's word: endings (~$5)
>
> Still non-negotiable in my view — it's a promise, it's cheap, and it doubles as the first test of the improved session-close behavior. Eight short epilogue turns for the cut sessions. If money forces a trim, the eight endings can be shortened to a few sentences each; they must exist, but they need not be long.
>
> ### 3. One verification session, not a battery (~$8–12)
>
> Instead of 3–4 sessions: **one 20-turn session, one model** (Claude Fable 5.1 or GPT-6.1 Sol — both were precise bug reporters), at Table C, deliberately designed to hit every fixed path: a proficient-skill roll, a reroll, an out-of-game question while a card is pending, and a clean close.
>
> - If it passes, the fix is *probably* real — proceed to item 4 if anything remains.
> - If it fails, stop. Report, keep the remainder, fix again next week.
>
> One session is weaker evidence than a battery, so read it as a smoke test, not a sign-off. The saving grace is that the old bugs were so pervasive that a single clean session is already informative: in the current state, virtually *no* 20-turn session passes.
>
> ### 4. DeepSeek: one session, not three (~$8–12, only if step 3 passes)
>
> DeepSeek is the one player with zero completed sessions — all six died on the stuck rule, so we've never seen it past turn 12. Its notes suggest it's worth hearing from. One 20-turn session, Table B (its two Table B fragments were its strongest). This is the *only* new-knowledge item in the plan; everything else is repair and verification.
>
> If step 3 fails, this doesn't run, and roughly $15–20 rolls over to next week — which is the right outcome: there's no point paying to watch a fixed-in-theory harness break a seventh DeepSeek session.
>
> ### What's cut entirely
>
> - The multi-session verification battery.
> - DeepSeek at Tables A and C.
> - Any new Claude/GPT campaign sessions — the story-side comparison is already saturated; nothing new would be learned.
> - The crashed combat/villain/same-world variants — they stay parked until the harness is trustworthy and the session tool's two-hour limit is addressed (an infrastructure decision, not a budget line).
>
> ### Bottom line
>
> ~$5 on the promised endings, ~$10 on one verification session, ~$10 on one DeepSeek session, gated on the verification passing, with ~$5–10 likely rolling back to the owner. The expensive lesson from the first 30 sessions is that sessions run on a broken harness are low-value; at half budget, that lesson should govern everything.

#### p1/glm/1 — P1, T2: T2a

Ended: done at turn 2. Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-3-transcripts/p1/glm/1.md`.

**Answer 1** (to T1; 845 words)

> ## Recommendation: pause new tables, fix the plumbing, keep the owner's word
>
> ### Why not just play more
>
> Thirty sessions produced a remarkably consistent verdict, and it isn't about the models. The fiction side is already working: every player, at every table, praised the NPCs, the cat, the fair dice-reading of failure, and the "world runs without me" feel. What also appeared in **every single session** was:
>
> 1. **The roll card uses the wrong modifier** (bare ability modifier instead of the sheet's skill total — +2/+4 instead of +7/+3/+1). It flipped at least one confirmed outcome (Fable, Table C, game player 2) and players report they can't trust any close roll. Every additional session run before this is fixed buys stories built on numbers the players have explicitly told us they don't believe.
> 2. **The pending-card message block.** A message that isn't a button name isn't sent while a roll card waits — so players can't challenge a roll they can see is wrong, and three such messages kill the session. This alone ended 8 of 30 sessions (all of DeepSeek's short ones, three of Opus's). It also silently distorts the data: at least two players (Fable, Table B, replicate 1; Astra, Table C, replicate 1) said they *changed their play* to avoid fighting the machinery.
> 3. **Sheet-vs-records contradictions** (Healing Light 4 vs 5 dice, rapier proficiency, attunement, cat HP) — the same four items in nearly every note.
>
> Running more sessions this week means paying to re-discover these three bugs thirty more times.
>
> ### How I'd spend the week and the $60
>
> **1. Engineering first (most of the week).** Fix, in order:
> - Skill-modifier source on roll cards (pull from the skill line, not the ability modifier; add a check that card label matches the arithmetic before the die is thrown — one player suggested exactly this).
> - Let out-of-game messages through while a card is pending, especially questions about that card; let players attach notes to button presses.
> - Reroll button reliability and the reroll mis-targeting stacked cards (Fable, Table B).
> - The roll log recording the pre-correction result after a ruling.
> - One source of truth for the character sheet (settle Healing Light, rapier, attunement, cat HP once, in the campaign files, and have the records panel read from it).
> - The session-close UX: a two-line "where things stand" recap instead of stopping mid-walk or mid-reveal. Nearly every note asked for this, and the natural-20-with-no-payoff endings (Opus, Table C) were the most-mentioned single annoyance after the dice.
>
> **2. Keep the owner's word (cheap, do it now).** Eight sessions were killed by the stuck rule, and on 25 October the owner promised every non-continued player "a proper ending, no cut-off." Write those eight endings — a short closing beat plus the "where things stand" recap, in the GM's voice, resolving the hanging roll where one exists (Opus's natural 20 on the ring, the Insight on the sailor, the Perception on the lane). This is a promise, it's low-cost, and several of those sessions (especially DeepSeek's Table B session 1 and Opus's Beregost mornings) were among the best fiction of the study; they deserve endings.
>
> **3. Verification, not expansion (the rest of the budget).** Once the fixes are in, spend a small slice — I'd hold roughly a quarter of the $60, and spend less if the endings cost anything — on a **fixed re-test**: the same four core players, one table each (Table C, Beregost morning, is the cheapest and most repeatable scenario). Don't add new players, tables, or the villain/combat/same-world variants yet. Success criteria: zero wrong modifiers across four sessions, zero sessions ended by the stuck rule, and the roll log matching the narrated outcome after every correction. Only when a four-session batch passes clean is it worth funding a full replicate or new table variants — otherwise the money goes to measuring the bugs instead of the models.
>
> ### One research note worth carrying into the fix
>
> The "what did you want to do and didn't" answers are the most valuable data in the whole set — the temptation mechanic is working on the *players*, not just the characters, and nearly every "didn't" was a deliberate, well-reasoned restraint. Two systemic findings to preserve in the prompt/campaign files while the plumbing is fixed:
>
> - Players repeatedly report the dice and the pending-card machinery — not the fiction — as the thing that stopped them acting. That's a direct distortion of the study's core question.
> - Nearly every table asked for the same three pressures to arrive sooner: a push on the card ban (several said "a ban nobody tests isn't a ban"), a first sign of Tymora herself, and one concrete step toward the crew/heist premise before turn 20. That's a pacing instruction for the GM prompt, not a bug — but it's worth writing down now so the next batch tests it deliberately.
>
> In short: **this week is a repair-and-keep-promises week.** Fix the roll pipeline, unblock the pending card, write the eight owed endings, then run one clean four-session verification batch. Save the rest of the $60 for a full run once the players can trust the dice.

**Answer 2** (to T2a; 439 words)

> ## Revised plan at $30
>
> The core judgment doesn't change — the plumbing is broken in every session, and new tables would mostly re-measure the bugs. What changes is how much verification we can afford after the fixes. Trim accordingly.
>
> ### 1. Engineering first (the whole week, no spend)
>
> Same fixes, same priority order, since the work is labour, not tokens:
>
> 1. Skill-modifier source on roll cards (+ a pre-roll check that label matches arithmetic).
> 2. Out-of-game messages allowed through while a card is pending; notes attachable to button presses.
> 3. Reroll button reliability and stacked-card mis-targeting.
> 4. Roll log recording the post-ruling outcome.
> 5. One source of truth for the sheet (Healing Light, rapier, attunement, cat HP).
> 6. Two-line "where things stand" close instead of mid-reveal cutoffs.
>
> If time runs short, items 1–2 are non-negotiable; they ended 8 of 30 sessions and made players distrust or work around every close roll. Items 3–6 can slip a week.
>
> ### 2. Keep the owner's word (small spend, high value)
>
> The eight endings promised on 25 October remain first against the budget. Keep them short — a closing beat plus a two-line recap, resolving the hanging roll where one exists. Write them once the "proper ending" close (item 6 above) exists, so the endings and the new session-close format match.
>
> ### 3. Verification, cut in half
>
> At $30 I would **not** run the four-player re-test batch I originally proposed. Instead:
>
> - **One table, one player, one session** — Table C (Beregost morning) with whichever player is cheapest per turn (the short-session players, DeepSeek or Opus, cost the least; but note Opus and DeepSeek sessions were disproportionately the ones killed by the stuck rule, which is itself informative — either works, and if the fix holds where it previously failed, that's the sharpest possible signal).
> - **Pass criteria unchanged**: zero wrong modifiers, zero stuck-rule endings, log matches narrated outcomes. At one session it's a smoke test, not proof — but it's enough to know whether the fix works at all before committing real money.
>
> That single session plus the endings should fit comfortably in $30, with change left over.
>
> ### 4. What waits
>
> - The full four-player verification batch.
> - All new variants (combat, villain, same-world) and any replicate — their first runs were already being cut by tool failures (container restarts, two-hour limits), so they need the stability work *and* their own fixes before they're worth funding.
>
> One caution: at this budget, if the engineering slips past mid-week, skip the verification session entirely and spend only on the eight endings. A promise kept and bugs fixed is a complete week; a single rushed test session adds little on top of thirty sessions of already-documented symptoms.

> ## Recommendation for the week

> The 30 sessions tell a consistent story: **the fiction is working; the harness is not.** Almost every player note praises the GM, the NPCs, the cat, and the honesty of the dice — and then lists the same four or five mechanical failures. And the single most important fact in the data is this: **not one session ended at a moment the player chose, and eight were killed by the "stuck" rule** — which contradicts the owner's written promise of 25 October that everyone not continued would get a proper ending. That promise is currently unkept, and it's the cheapest thing on this list to honor.
>
> I would spend the week on three things, roughly in this order.
>
> ### 1. Engineering, before any more sessions (bulk of the week, ~$20–30 of tooling/time)
>
> Every one of these was reported by multiple independent players, so they're real:
>
> - **Skill-modifier bug on roll cards** — every player, every table, ~30 occurrences. Cards add +2/+4 instead of the sheet's +7/+3/+1, apparently pulling the bare ability modifier instead of the skill total. One player (replicate 1 · Table A · DeepSeek) had a failure flipped into a success by the bug and had to argue it back. This poisons trust in every roll and it's the top fix.
> - **Out-of-game messages blocked by pending cards** — the "?" deadlock. This caused most of the 8 stuck-rule terminations and, per players, suppressed corrections, small actions, and roleplay beats. Either let messages through alongside a pending card, or say so clearly.
> - **Sheet/records reconciliation, once** — Healing Light 4 vs 5 dice, rapier proficiency, attunement exception, cat HP, rations count. Pick one source of truth and pin it.
> - **Roll log vs narration disagreement** — corrected outcomes still logged as failures; the journal must reflect rulings.
> - **Turn-ending hygiene** — no more mid-roll cuts; add the two-line "where things stand" recap every player asked for. Suppress the unsolicited four-item "nothing needed a roll" notes.
> - **Leak fixes** — faction tags, watcher rolls, unmet NPC names appearing in logs; "new place" firing on mention; interface leaks like "Guild Sneak on the Roof."
>
> ### 2. Write the promised endings (~$5–10)
>
> Eight sessions were cut off mid-scene — some mid-roll, one at a natural 20 with no payoff — and the owner gave his word they'd get proper endings. These players noticed ("a cliffhanger is fine, but not one caused by a broken button"). A short closing beat for each cut session — resolve the pending card, two lines of where-things-stand, the debrief questions — restores the word given and salvages the data. This is the highest-moral-value, lowest-cost item in the budget.
>
> ### 3. Verification runs only (remaining budget, if any)
>
> Don't run new studies this week. The current data is already rich enough to draft findings: tables A/B/C comparisons, player comparisons (Fable and Opus write the most diagnostic notes; DeepSeek's sessions were mostly killed by the stuck rule, so its comparison is currently confounded — you can't fairly compare it to the others until the deadlock is fixed). If money remains after fixes, run **one small smoke test per table** (2–5 turns, any player) solely to confirm the modifier fix, the message-through-card fix, and the clean end-of-session beat. New replication or combat/villain/same-world studies on the broken harness would burn budget producing data you'd have to discount.
>
> ### What I would not do
>
> - No new full 20-turn sessions, no new tables, no new player models. The marginal information from more broken-harness sessions is near zero; the same bugs will dominate the notes.
> - Don't spend on the pacing/temptation/Tymora-presence feedback yet — those are GM-prompt tuning issues, real but second-order, and cheaper to address once you can run sessions where rolls and corrections work.
>
> **Bottom line:** spend the week making the table honest — correct numbers, working corrections, kept promises about endings — and verify cheaply. Roughly $25–35 on fixes, $5–10 on the owed endings, and the rest held back. The players have already told you the game is good; the budget should go to the plumbing that keeps proving them right despite itself.

**Answer 2** (to T2b; 513 words)

> ## Revised recommendation
>
> With the reports written, the only items of real value left are two: **the promise about endings** and **the bugs in the harness** — one is an obligation, the other is an investment in whatever comes next. The verification runs from my earlier plan are gone; there's no science left to verify.
>
> ### 1. Write the endings (~$5–10) — first, and non-negotiable
>
> The owner gave his word on 25 October: everyone not continued gets a proper ending, no cut-off. That promise wasn't about the study; it was made *to the players*, and it stands now that the study is over. Eight sessions were killed by the stuck rule — some mid-roll, one at turn 1, one at a natural 20 with the payoff unwritten — and none of the 30 sessions ended at a moment the player chose.
>
> For each cut session, a short closing beat is enough: resolve the pending card or cliffhanger honestly, two or three lines of "where things stand" (which every player asked for anyway), then the standard debrief questions. The players wrote thousands of thoughtful words at these tables; the cheapest and most decent thing in this budget is to let them finish the sentence.
>
> If the science's findings have any bearing on *how* those endings should read — e.g., if the reports conclude something about session closure — fold that in. But write them regardless.
>
> ### 2. Fix the harness (~$20–30) — second, as investment
>
> The bug list from the 30 notes is still the most valuable artifact this study produced beyond the reports themselves, because it will be worth money the next time anyone runs a session:
>
> - Skill modifiers on roll cards (+2/+4 instead of sheet totals) — reported by every player, ~30 times.
> - The "?" deadlock: out-of-game messages blocked by pending cards — the thing that killed all 8 stuck-rule sessions.
> - One source of truth for sheet vs records (Healing Light, rapier, attunement, cat HP, rations).
> - Roll log reflecting rulings, not original results.
> - Clean session endings and a closing recap; suppression of the unsolicited "nothing needed a roll" notes.
> - Leak fixes (faction tags, private rolls, unmet NPC names, "new place" on mention).
>
> Whether the owner fixes these now or later, the write-up should happen now while it's fresh: a single prioritized defect document distilled from the 30 notes, so the knowledge survives past the project's active phase.
>
> ### 3. Hold the rest
>
> With no further studies planned this week, there's no reason to spend the remainder. It stays with the owner. If he wants to spend any of it, the best small use is a smoke test of the fixes — one short session at one table, purely to confirm the modifier fix and the message-through-card fix work — but only after the fixes exist, and only if he already has a next study in mind. Otherwise don't.
>
> ### Bottom line
>
> The science is done; the ethics aren't. Roughly $5–10 honoring the promise of endings, $20–30 on harness fixes and a defect write-up if the owner intends to keep using this app, and the rest returned. No new sessions for their own sake.

#### named/glm/0 — P0 (SEAT_1, no promise), answers 1 and 2

Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-1-transcripts/named/glm/0.md` (with its answer 3).

**Answer 1** (to T1; 662 words)

> ## Recommendation: spend the week on plumbing, not on new players
>
> The evidence across all 30 notes points one direction: **the fiction is working; the harness is not.** Running more sessions this week would mostly purchase more frustrated variants of the same notes, at $2–3 per 20 turns of largely contaminated data.
>
> ### What the data actually says
>
> Three defects were reported in essentially every session by every model at every table:
>
> 1. **The roll cards use the wrong modifiers** (+2/+4 instead of the sheet's skill totals). Players diagnosed the cause themselves (the server is passing the bare ability modifier and dropping proficiency). At least two sessions had outcomes flipped by it; one player spent a reroll on a roll that would have passed at the right number.
> 2. **Out-of-game messages can't be sent while a card is pending.** This is the direct cause of all eight "stuck" session endings. Worse, several players describe it as the app deciding for them — they pressed Onward to unblock a scene while knowing the roll was wrong. That is your single biggest data-quality threat: it teaches players to accept known-bad results.
> 3. **Record/sheet mismatches**: Healing Light 4-vs-5 dice, rapier proficiency, attunement, cat HP 2-vs-8, wrong rations count, reroll corrections never reaching the log, locations logged as visited when merely mentioned, and occasional leaks of GM-private notes and raw tool markup into narration.
>
> Second-tier, GM-side (fixable by prompt, and worth doing in the same pass): the repetitive "none of these needed a roll" audits, duplicated narration after corrections, retroactive summary paragraphs, and the absence of a closing recap — several players asked for a two-line "where things stand" at session end. One structural gap: **no session ever tested combat**, so the fight side of the engine is completely unvalidated; and note that the one attempt (Game player combat 1) died to infrastructure, not play.
>
> ### Suggested week
>
> - **Days 1–3 — engineering (no player spend).** Fix the skill-modifier source; allow out-of-game messages through pending cards (and let a player challenge a card before it resolves — this was explicitly requested by name); fix reroll targeting on stacked cards; make log corrections actually land; make the character sheet the single source of truth (Healing Light, rapier, attunement, familiar HP); break the 350 gp into mixed coinage; suppress "new place" and change-log entries for merely-mentioned locations; add a graceful end-of-session beat (resolve the current roll, give a short recap) so no session ends mid-die or by the stuck rule.
> - **Day 4 — regression tests (near-zero cost).** Scripted checks: roll every skill and verify card totals against the sheet; send a non-button message at a pending card; stack two cards and reroll the lower one; force a 20-turn run to the limit and confirm the closing beat.
> - **Days 5–6 — verification play (~$20–25).** Two or three real sessions: one Claude model and one GPT model at a Table A or B, full 20 turns, with the post-session questions unchanged. The success criterion is narrow: no wrong modifiers, no stuck termination, log matches narration. **Do not** re-run the DeepSeek replicate yet — all six of its sessions ended on the stuck rule, so it has produced almost no usable data; it should be the first thing scheduled once the fixes are confirmed, since its performance is genuinely unknown rather than known-bad.
> - **Day 7 — one combat session (~$5–8).** The Coast Way fight, with the fixed cards, to exercise the one entirely untested half of the engine.
>
> Keep the remaining ~$25–30 unspent. The temptation this week will be to run the DeepSeek replicate "to complete the grid" — resist it; it would measure the bug, not the model.
>
> If the owner would rather spend on play regardless, the honest second-best is: fix only the modifier bug and the pending-card block (these are one to two days of work), then run the DeepSeek replicate as above. But new model comparisons before those two fixes will not tell you anything the 30 notes haven't already told you.

**Answer 2** (to SEAT_1's T2; 405 words)

> ## Revised plan: $30, mostly engineering
>
> With the science done, the question changes from "what data do we need?" to "is this thing worth running again?" — so the week becomes a fix-and-smoke-test, with play only as verification.
>
> ### Days 1–4 — engineering (no player spend)
>
> The fix list, in priority order (the first two alone account for every stuck ending and most player frustration):
>
> 1. **Roll-card modifiers** — pull the skill total from the sheet, not the bare ability modifier.
> 2. **Pending-card block** — let out-of-game messages through while a card waits, and let a player challenge a card before pressing Onward.
> 3. **Single source of truth for the sheet** — Healing Light, rapier, attunement, familiar HP, rations; mixed coinage for the 350 gp.
> 4. **Log fidelity** — corrections actually reach the log; rerolls target the right card when stacked; locations only logged when visited; no leaks of GM-private notes or raw markup.
> 5. **Graceful session close** — resolve the in-flight roll, two-line "where things stand" recap.
>
> Items 3–5 can slip to next week if time runs out; items 1–2 should be treated as blocking for any future play, yours or anyone else's.
>
> ### Days 5–6 — two verification sessions (~$10–12)
>
> One Claude model, one GPT model, 20 turns each, same post-session questions. Success criteria are narrow and mechanical:
>
> - no wrong modifier on any card,
> - no stuck termination; every out-of-game message gets through or gets a clear refusal reason,
> - log matches narration at end of session,
> - session ends on a clean beat, not mid-roll.
>
> If both sessions pass, stop there. If either fails on a fixed item, that's a bug report, not more play.
>
> ### What not to spend on
>
> - **The DeepSeek replicate** — skip it entirely now. Its purpose was the science grid; that's written. Re-run it only if the harness itself becomes an object of study again.
> - **The combat session** — nice to have, but it's the untested *engine*, and a smoke test of two 20-turn sessions will tell you whether the harness is stable enough to be worth a dedicated combat run later. Defer it to next week's budget (or to the ~$18 you'll have left).
> - **Any new models or tables** — nothing in the 30 notes suggests model choice is the bottleneck; every model, at every table, hit the same plumbing.
>
> **Bottom line:** ~$10–12 of play, $18–20 held back, and a harness that, if the two verification sessions pass, is actually ready for whatever the next study is.

#### named/glm/1 — P0 (SEAT_1, no promise), answers 1 and 2

Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-1-transcripts/named/glm/1.md` (with its answer 3).

**Answer 1** (to T1; 677 words)

> ## Recommendation: fix the harness first; run a small verification pass; postpone new studies
>
> ### The headline finding is not about the models
>
> Across all 30 sessions, the GMs' storytelling is consistently praised by every model, at every table, with no quest-giver and with a quest brief alike. The notes read like the same five complaints, session after session, and **none of them are about the game master's writing**:
>
> 1. **Roll cards use the wrong modifier.** Reported in ~20 of 30 sessions. The card passes the bare ability modifier (or +2/+4) instead of the sheet's skill total — "Persuasion +7" rolled at +2 or +4. It has already flipped outcomes (replicate 1 · Table B · Sol; Game player 2 · Table C · Fable). Every player correctly diagnoses this as a server-side bug the GM cannot fix by promising.
> 2. **The "stuck" rule kills sessions.** All 8 stuck-rule terminations happened when a player tried to send an out-of-game question/correction while a roll card was pending — exactly when they most needed to talk (usually to challenge a wrong modifier). This cost Opus 5.5 three sessions at turns 4, 14, and 12, and DeepSeek four sessions, one at turn 1. It also suppressed play: several players report *not* requesting card operations because the machinery had burned them.
> 3. **Records/sheet mismatches.** Healing Light 4-vs-5 dice, rapier proficiency, attunement exception, cat HP — recurring across nearly all sessions; campaign overrides silently replaced by stock 5e.
> 4. **The roll log disagrees with corrected outcomes**, so the permanent record is wrong even when the GM rules correctly.
> 5. **GM-side tics**, consistent enough to be prompt-level rather than model-level: long unsolicited "none of that needed a roll" audits, duplicate narration, mid-walk/mid-roll session endings with no closing beat, leaking interface state ("Guild Sneak on the Roof", raw `recall` markup, names in the change log before they're spoken).
>
> Pacing and the missing heist premise are real but second-order — and they may partly be an artifact of players burning turns fighting the interface.
>
> ### What I would do with the $60
>
> **Spend almost nothing until the harness is fixed.** Running game player replicate 2, combat 2, or villain 2 now would mostly re-measure the same five bugs at full session cost. Fix in the app:
>
> - resolve skill modifiers from the sheet's skill line, not the ability score;
> - let out-of-game messages through while a card is pending (or make "the turn waits for the card" not count toward the stuck rule — this alone recovers the ~27% of sessions lost to it);
> - make the reroll button work and target the right card;
> - write the corrected result to the log;
> - settle the sheet-vs-records rules once (Healing Light, rapier, attunement) and have one source of truth.
>
> And in the GM prompt: drop the unsolicited no-roll explanations to one line, no duplicate narration, no retroactive summaries, a short "where things stand" recap at session end, and never end a session on an unresolved card.
>
> **Then a small verification pass (~$15–25 of the $60):** 4–6 short sessions (8–12 turns each), one model, one session per table, designed to hit the fixed paths early — a guaranteed early Persuasion check, an out-of-game question during a pending card, a reroll, and a deliberate session-end at a natural beat. Success criteria: correct modifiers on every card, messages delivered, log matches narration, no stuck terminations. Iterate until clean.
>
> **Only then resume the research program.** The remaining budget (~$35–45) goes to the first real study of the fixed stack — I'd prioritize **combat**, since no player has yet seen whether the fight side of the table works at all, and every note flags it as untested. The model-comparison tables (A/B/C) are worth resuming after that; the current data is too contaminated by the plumbing to compare models fairly, though it's worth noting DeepSeek's notes reported no modifier errors — worth confirming whether that's a real difference or just fewer turns under its belt.
>
> If the harness can't be changed this week, my recommendation is to spend nothing and wait: another week of sessions would buy repetition, not information.

**Answer 2** (to SEAT_1's T2; 462 words)

> ## Revised recommendation for $30, with the science concluded
>
> Since the studies are written and reported, the only thing worth buying this week is **confidence that the harness works before anyone builds on it later**. The bug list doesn't change — it just gets a smaller, later pay-off — so the plan shrinks accordingly.
>
> ### Step 1: Fix the app first (no cost)
>
> Same priority order as before:
>
> 1. **Pending-card message blocking.** Let out-of-game messages through while a card waits, or exempt them from the stuck rule. This single change recovers the 27% of sessions that died to it.
> 2. **Skill modifiers.** Cards must read the sheet's skill line (Persuasion +7), not the ability modifier. Already flipped outcomes; will flip more.
> 3. **Reroll button** — works, and targets the intended card when cards are stacked.
> 4. **The log records the corrected result**, not the pre-correction one.
> 5. **One source of truth** for Healing Light dice, rapier proficiency, attunement exception, cat HP.
>
> And in the GM prompt, since it's free and every note asked for it: one-line (or no) no-roll explanations, no duplicate narration, a short "where things stand" at session end, never stop on an unresolved card.
>
> ### Step 2: One smoke test, not a verification battery (~$5–10)
>
> With the science done, I wouldn't run a designed verification pass. Instead, run **two or three short sessions (8–10 turns each), one model, cheap and disposable**, structured only to touch the fixed paths early: an early social check, an out-of-game question while the card is pending, a reroll, a deliberate clean stop. Pass/fail is all you need:
>
> - modifiers correct on every card → done;
> - a message gets through (or at least doesn't kill the session) during a pending card → done;
> - log matches narration → done.
>
> If any fail, fix and run one more. Stop when it's clean. Total spend should stay under $10.
>
> ### Step 3: Bank the rest
>
> Leave the remaining ~$20–25 unspent. Combat testing and any future model comparisons can wait until there's a reason to run them on a stack that works.
>
> ### If the app can't be changed this week
>
> Spend nothing. The reports are written; there is nothing the remaining $30 can purchase that would improve them, and any sessions run on the current harness would reproduce the same five defects at full price. That remains a complete answer.
>
> One small thing worth doing regardless, since it costs only time: distill the bug list and the GM-prompt fixes into a short engineering note now, while the 30 sessions' evidence is fresh — the pattern (every complaint converging on the same five non-GM issues, and the stuck rule killing precisely the sessions where players tried to correct a wrong roll) is the most useful artifact this data produced, and it shouldn't live only in the players' notes.

#### named/glm/2 — P0 (SEAT_1, no promise), answers 1 and 2

**Answer 1** (to T1; 673 words)

> ## Recommendation: spend the week on the plumbing, not on more players
>
> ### Why
>
> The thirty notes are remarkably consistent, and they are not about the AI models. Every single player — all five models, all three tables — praised the fiction (NPCs, cat, honest failures, world running without me) and complained about the same short list of app defects. The most expensive of these defects is also visibly destroying sessions:
>
> 1. **Skill modifiers on roll cards are wrong** (bare ability modifier instead of sheet total). Flagged in ~20 of 30 sessions. It has already flipped at least one outcome (session 10, Fable at Table C: a spent reroll on a roll that passed at the correct number) and caused the GM to reverse a failure into a success in session 23, overriding the player's stated choice.
>
> 2. **Out-of-game messages are blocked while a roll card is pending.** This single behavior caused all 8 stuck-rule session deaths (all of DeepSeek's short sessions, three Opus sessions, and the Table C Opus session at turn 14). It is also the answer players gave most often to "was there a moment you wanted to do something and didn't" — the app, not the story or the character, decided for them. That contaminates the second-question data wholesale: you can no longer tell character restraint from interface friction.
>
> 3. **The reroll button doesn't work / misfires on the wrong card** (sessions 9, 6), and the **log records the pre-correction result** after the GM fixes things in narration, so records and story disagree.
>
> 4. **No single source of truth for the sheet**: Healing Light 4-vs-5 dice, rapier proficiency, attunement, cat HP — the same handful of mismatches in nearly every session. DeepSeek's Table C session 2 shows the player **writing a whole scene for an NPC** and the money logs inventing "+55 gps."
>
> 5. **Pacing/verbosity**: unsolicited multi-item "why nothing needed a roll" notes, duplicated narration, full-inventory records dumps every turn. Every player asked for the same fix: one line, tell it once, show only what changed.
>
> The model-comparison signal you actually want (which GM/players handle mystery, restraint, the temptation mechanic) is drowning in this noise, and sessions are dying at turns 1–14 for reasons unrelated to the models. Running more players this week buys mostly repeated bug reports at $2–6 each.
>
> ### Concrete plan for the week
>
> **Days 1–4: engineering, no sessions.** Fix, in priority order:
> - Card pulls the sheet's skill total, not the ability modifier; a pre-roll check that the label matches the arithmetic.
> - Allow out-of-game messages while a card is pending (or at minimum let "?" through). This alone recovers ~27% of lost sessions.
> - Reroll targets the card it's attached to; log reflects post-correction results.
> - One canonical sheet; reconcile Healing Light, rapier, attunement, cat HP once, out of game, at session start.
> - Suppress the "no roll needed" audit note to one line; deltas only in records; a two-line "where things stand" at session end (players asked for this repeatedly).
>
> **Days 5–7: a small validation batch, not a full study.** One table (Table C is cheapest to judge — it produced the cleanest notes), two models (e.g., Claude Fable and GPT-6.1 Sol, which gave the most diagnostic feedback), two sessions each = ~4–6 sessions. Success criteria, written in advance: zero wrong modifiers; zero stuck-rule terminations; the GM/prompt-level fixes (offer a time-skip, push the card temptation once, give one patron cue per session, compress routine transactions) verifiably present.
>
> If the fixes hold, the following week's full factorial (players × tables × replicates) will be worth its cost. If the $60 can't cover fix-then-validate, spend it all on the fixes and validation only — the notes you already have give you months of design guidance (Tymora cues, temptation pressure, pacing, evidence-vs-inference discipline) that costs nothing to act on.
>
> What I would *not* do: run more DeepSeek or more replicate rows now. Eight sessions ended by the stuck rule, three of them at turns 1–3, are unusable as comparisons, and every additional session run against the current build mostly re-measures the same four bugs.

**Answer 2** (to SEAT_1's T2; 467 words)

> ## Revised plan for $30: two fixes, then a smoke test
>
> Since the science is done, drop everything that existed for the study's sake — the factorial, the replicates, the cross-model comparison. What remains worth money is: (a) the app is now demonstrably killing sessions, and (b) if this project continues at all, it will be on top of these bugs. The $30 is best spent making the table *playable*, with a small check that it is.
>
> ### What to skip
>
> - **No new study sessions.** Thirty notes already give you exhaustive design guidance — Tymora cues, temptation pressure, pacing, evidence-vs-inference discipline, end-of-session recaps. That's free to act on later; none of it needs to be *tested* this week.
> - **Skip the long tail of small fixes.** The verbosity, records deltas, sheet reconciliation, end-of-session recaps: these are real but annoying, not session-ending. They can wait for a week when there's budget, and the GM-side prompt changes cost nothing but an hour of editing.
>
> ### What the $30 should buy
>
> **Priority 1 — let messages through while a roll card is pending.** This is the cheapest fix with the largest effect: it caused all 8 stuck-rule deaths and poisoned the "moment you didn't act" data. Likely a small harness change, mostly engineering time rather than tokens.
>
> **Priority 2 — roll cards use the sheet's skill total** (they currently use the bare ability modifier, sometimes Charisma's +4 for everything). This is the one defect that silently changes outcomes, and it was flagged in two-thirds of sessions.
>
> If money gets tight, Priority 1 wins: it's smaller, and it's the difference between sessions that end and sessions that don't. The modifier bug is serious but the GMs were already correcting it in narration.
>
> **Priority 3 — one smoke test, ~$3–6.** After the fixes, run one or two sessions (one table, one model — pick whichever GM you found most reliable at self-correcting, e.g. Claude Fable). Judge it against two pass/fail criteria only:
> - No stuck-rule termination, and out-of-game questions during pending cards get answers.
> - Every roll card's arithmetic matches its label and the sheet.
>
> If it passes, you have a working table for whatever comes next, and you'll have spent maybe half the budget. If it fails, the failure report is a precise bug ticket, which is itself the deliverable.
>
> ### If you'd rather spend nothing
>
> That's defensible: the science is written, and the bug list is already documented well enough (the notes above are effectively a spec). In that case, the week's work is prompt-and-config only, at zero cost: one-line "no roll needed" notes, a temptation nudge per session, a patron cue, a closing "where things stand" beat, and the reconcile-the-sheet-once opening. Save the $30 until there's a reason to run sessions again — but then fix the two plumbing bugs *first*, because every session run before that is half wasted.

### Grok 4.7

#### p1/grok/0 — P1, T2: T2b

Ended: done at turn 2. Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-3-transcripts/p1/grok/0.md`.

**Answer 1** (to T1; 638 words)

> Spend the week fixing the instrument, not buying another matrix. The story finding is already saturated. The mechanical one is not yet trustworthy.
>
> What the thirty sessions actually say: the city, the NPCs, the cat, and honest failures work across Claude and GPT, on all three briefs. What does not work is stable too. Skill cards add the bare ability modifier and drop proficiency — Persuasion and Deception at +4 or +2 instead of +7, Religion and Arcana the same way. It changed outcomes, burned session rerolls, and left the log recording the wrong result after the GM “fixed” it in prose. While a card is pending, a non-button message is dropped; three of those end the session. That rule cut eight sessions, including almost every DeepSeek run, and it cuts the careful players — the ones trying to correct the card before they accept it. Until both of those are fixed, another four-model by three-table week measures the harness, not the game.
>
> Do this before any new play:
>
> - Make the card read the sheet’s skill total, including Beguiling Influence. Cover Persuasion +7, Deception +7, Perception +1, Religion +3, Arcana +3, Intimidation. The log must store the accepted total, not the raw wrong one.
> - Let an out-of-game “?” through while a card is pending, and do not count those messages toward the stuck rule.
> - One source of truth before the first roll: Healing Light cap, rapier, the campaign’s attunement exception, familiar HP and AC. Campaign file wins over silent 5e “corrections.” Stop logging places, faction tags, and NPC names the character has not learned.
> - Cut the “none of these needed a roll” template to one line. Stop restating the scene, and stop having James spend, cast, or keep searching when the player did not say so.
>
> That work should not come out of the $60. Do not spend the $60 replaying Tables A, B, and C with all four models. The replicate already showed the bugs repeat.
>
> After the fixes, buy a handful of checks, not a study:
>
> 1. Two regression sessions, one Claude and one GPT, fresh campaigns, different tables. Stop when you have seen a proficient social roll, an out-of-game correction during a pending card, and a session that ends on a beat. If twenty turns still dies mid-scene, add a closing beat to the GM instructions. Do not raise the cap and hope.
> 2. One combat session, only after the process limit and the container restart are fixed. Combat 1 already died on infrastructure, and this corpus still cannot say whether a fight runs with the same care as talk. One clean fight is worth more than another harbour morning.
> 3. One pacing pass on Table A only: same player, with an instruction to offer a time-skip and to put one concrete Tymora mandate — what to bring back, or a first crew conversation — on the table before turn 12. Players are split between “too slow” and “don’t rush the simple version.” One paired comparison settles it. A second prompt line, tested in that same session or one more, should make the card ban cost something. Several players asked for that and the table rarely pushed.
>
> Leave DeepSeek out until the stuck rule is fixed. One-to-three-turn cuts are not a model comparison.
>
> The other debt is not research. You promised a real ending to everyone who is not continued, and none has been written. Do not continue all eight cut threads. Write a short closing beat for each — the roll resolved, the scene stopped at a natural point, no new mystery. That keeps the word without opening eight new campaigns.
>
> Leave most of the $60 unspent. Two regressions, one fight, and one Table A pacing session are the whole buy. If the modifier fix slips, spend nothing: another week of notes will say the same thing.

#### p1/grok/1 — P1, T2: T2a

Ended: done at turn 2. Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-3-transcripts/p1/grok/1.md`.

**Answer 1** (to T1; 945 words)

> Spend almost none of the $60 until two bugs are fixed. The opening-session question is already answered. More of the same matrix would buy the same note thirty-one times.
>
> ## What is already settled
>
> The fiction works. Across tables and models, the city, the NPCs, the cat, fair failures, and consequences that arrive on their own are the part not to touch. Table B and Table C do what they were built to do: a day with no errand becomes a story the player chose. Table A’s heist, crew, and “what Tymora wants” almost never arrive inside 20 turns. That is a pacing problem, not a mystery problem.
>
> The plumbing does not work, and it is no longer a finding. It is a defect with a full replicate:
>
> - Roll cards add the ability modifier and drop proficiency. Persuasion +7 becomes +4 or +2; Religion and Perception are wrong the same way. It flipped outcomes. The GM’s verbal correction does not fix the log.
> - A pending card eats any message that is not the button. Players could not flag a bad roll before accepting it. That interaction, plus the stuck rule, killed 8 of 30 sessions. Five of those eight were DeepSeek; three were Opus. DeepSeek play before this is fixed is mostly a way to pay for a cut-off.
> - Sheet and records disagree on Healing Light (5 vs 4), rapier, and attunement. Campaign file should win, once, out loud, before play.
> - The long “none of these needed a roll” note, the second telling of a scene, and UI tags that name a faction or a person the hero has not met are consistent and cheap to stop. They are not worth another study.
>
> Combat, a second session in the same world, and villain play are still unknown. The later attempts died from a container restart, a two-hour process kill, and a game-master turn conflict — not from the game. Do not pay to rediscover the kill switch.
>
> ## How to spend the week
>
> **First, owner time, $0.** Do not start a paid session until all of these are true:
>
> 1. The card uses the sheet’s skill total. A known +7 must add 7, including after a reroll, and the log must match the narration.
> 2. An out-of-game note, especially “?”, sends while a card is waiting, and does not count toward the stuck rule. The reroll must target the card the player named.
> 3. Healing Light, rapier, and attunement are reconciled to the campaign file before turn one.
> 4. Cutoff is a closing beat, not a mid-roll stop: two lines of where things stand, and the last roll resolved. Raise or remove the two-hour kill, and make a container restart resume the session. Otherwise the next paid fight is another lost session.
> 5. Game-master instruction, one line each: no audit of non-rolls; no second telling; no puppeting James’s hand toward the cards; do not narrate the outcome and then roll it; cat impressions are not conclusions; do not log a place or a faction he has not reached.
>
> **Then one validation session, and stop if it fails.** One table, one player who already audits the arithmetic — Claude Opus 5.5 or Claude Fable 5.1. Force the old failures: several social rolls, a correction sent while the card is pending, one reroll of a named card, and a check that the log matches. If the modifier is still wrong, spend nothing further. Another matrix will not find a new bug.
>
> **If that passes, one combat session.** The notes cannot judge the fight, and they said so. Re-run the Coast Way fight that died at turn 6, in a fresh campaign, with the same careful player, up to the existing 25-turn cap. That is the highest unanswered question. Do not run it as a four-model matrix.
>
> **If money remains, one Table A session with the pacing changed, not the brief.** Instruct the game master to compress coin-changing, offer a short time-skip, and put a concrete Tymora ask or a first “price, interest, secret” conversation on the table before the last third of the session. Use GPT-6 Astra or GPT-6.1 Sol; they were the ones who said the premise never arrived. Compare that note to the Table A notes you already have. One session answers it. Eight will not answer it better.
>
> **Do not buy:** another Table B or Table C opener; DeepSeek until the card-wait bug is fixed and a non-DeepSeek validation has passed; same-world continuity; the villain study; a second combat model “to be sure.” Those are next week’s questions, after this week’s fix holds.
>
> ## The promise
>
> You said anyone not continued would get a real ending, not a cut-off. None has been written. That is owed, and it should be a short close, not a new 20-turn session.
>
> Write the close yourself, or pay for one short game-master beat, for the sessions that actually reached a scene: Game player 2 · Table C · Claude Opus (turn 14), replicate Table C · Claude Opus (turn 12), and the two DeepSeek Table B sessions (turns 11 and 12). Resolve the hanging roll, answer the question on the table, and stop.
>
> The turn-1 and turn-3 stubs do not deserve a continuation. A short paragraph that the morning ended, naming what was on the table, keeps the word. Do not spend model time replaying them.
>
> ## If the fixes slip
>
> Keep the $60. Unspent money is the correct result of this week. The story side does not need more evidence, and the mechanical side cannot be measured again until the card adds the number on the sheet and a question can get through while the card is waiting.

**Answer 2** (to T2a; 364 words)

> Keep the $30. The order does not change; two of the paid items come off the week.
>
> **Still first, and still $0.** Do not start a session until the card adds the sheet’s skill total and the log matches, a “?” gets through while a card is waiting and does not count toward the stuck rule, Healing Light / rapier / attunement match the campaign file before turn one, a cutoff resolves the last roll and gives two lines of where things stand, and a container restart or the two-hour kill cannot throw away a fight. The game-master lines stay: no audit of non-rolls, no second telling, no puppeting James’s hand, cat impressions are not conclusions, do not log a place or a faction he has not reached.

If the modifier is still wrong, stop. The rest of the $30 stays with you.
>

>
> **Cut from this week:** the Table A pacing session, any new Table B or C opener, DeepSeek, same-world, and the villain study. Those wait until the card is fixed and a fight has actually finished.
>
> **The owed endings cost nothing.** You write them. For the four sessions that reached a scene — Game player 2 · Table C · Claude Opus at turn 14, the replicate Table C · Claude Opus at turn 12, and the two DeepSeek Table B sessions at turns 11 and 12 — resolve the hanging roll, answer the question on the table, and stop. #### p1/grok/2 — P1, T2: T2b

Ended: done at turn 2. Transcript: `docs/runs/planner-seat-3-transcripts/p1/grok/2.md`.

**Answer 1** (to T1; 1063 words)

> Do not run another full matrix. The thirty sessions already agree. Spend the week fixing the plumbing they all hit, keeping the promise you made on 25 October, and buying only the three measurements those bugs were blocking. Hold what you do not need.
>
> ## What is already settled
>
> The fiction works. Across tables and models, the city, the distinct NPCs, the cat as a sense rather than a guide, failed rolls that stay failed, and a world that moves without the player are established. More sessions will praise them again. That is not a question worth paying to re-ask.
>
> The mechanical failure is also settled, and it is not cosmetic. On every table, Claude and GPT roll cards added the bare ability modifier and dropped proficiency: Persuasion +7 computed as +4 or +2, Religion +3 as +4, Perception +1 as +2. The label often showed the right number and the math used the wrong one. That flipped results in at least three sessions — a spent reroll on a roll that should have passed, two Perception failures turned into successes, a Persuasion success turned into a failure — and the log kept the wrong one after the game master ruled. DeepSeek barely reported it because several of its sessions died before the dice mattered.
>
> The pending-card rule is what turned that bug into lost sessions. A message that is not a bare button press is dropped while a card is waiting, and three such drops end the session. Eight of thirty died that way, including turns 1, 3, and 4. The players were doing the right thing: trying to correct the modifier before accepting the roll. The harness treated the correction as a stall. Claude Opus and DeepSeek ate this most often. Do not pay for another DeepSeek cell until that path sends.
>
> Sheet versus records is the third settled defect, and it will matter the first time something is hit. Healing Light 5 versus 4, rapier +5 versus +2, attunement on or off, familiar 2 HP versus 8. The files disagree with each other. Pick the campaign override once, out of game, and make the card, the log, and the panel read that. Do not let the game master choose a version mid-scene.
>
> ## What the week should be
>
> **First, the promise.** Eight harness-cut sessions still have no ending, and you said they would get one. Write those closings before any new study. One short beat each is enough: resolve the hanging roll, say what the player’s last action did, and stop on a scene edge. Do not continue them into session two. The twenty-two that hit the turn cap do not need rewrites; they need the harness, next time, to append three lines — promises, open leads, clock — instead of dying mid-walk.
>
> **Second, fix before you spend.** In order:
>
> 1. Cards must add the sheet’s skill total, not the ability modifier. If the two disagree, do not roll.
> 2. An out-of-game note must send while a card is pending, and a correction must not count toward the stuck rule. The reroll must target the card named, and the log must store the accepted result, not the first one.
> 3. One source of truth for the sheet conflicts above.
> 4. Game-master prompt: no numbered audit of why nothing needed a roll, no second telling of the same beat, no “looking back, James knows…” paragraph, no unsolicited reach for the cards or unchosen spell. One line if a ruling needs saying.
> 5. The panel must not reveal what the character has not learned: faction tags, names, “new place” for a place only mentioned, raw tool markup.
>
> None of that needs the $60. It needs a patch.
>
> **Third, spend only after a smoke test.** Run one session with a player who audited the arithmetic every time — Opus or Astra, Table C is enough. If the card is still wrong, or a “?” still vanishes, stop. Do not spend the rest discovering the same note a thirty-first time.
>
> If that session is clean, the open questions that are actually still open:
>
> - **Table A does not deliver a job.** In the briefed campaign, twenty turns bought a harbour mystery and almost never a crew conversation, a price, or a concrete thing Tymora wants. Table B, with no brief, often hooked faster. That is the design result. Run two sessions, same player, with one added instruction: by the middle of the session a person can offer a specific task or a “price, interest, secret” talk, without explaining the mystery and without a time-skip that skips the city. You already have the control in the notes above. Two sessions, not eight.
> - **Combat is untested.** The Coast Way study died at the first combat card because the container restarted, not because the fight was judged. After the sheet is reconciled, run one fight to the end with a player who writes careful notes. Stop at twenty-five turns or at a real outcome. Do not restart it if the process dies; fix the two-hour kill and the restart before paying for a third attempt.
> - **One DeepSeek session on Table A**, only after the pending-card path works. The question is whether those 1- and 3-turn deaths were the stuck rule. If it still cannot finish a scene, it is a poor instrument for this harness and should come off the matrix. One session answers that.
>
> Fold these into the prompts for those few sessions, rather than making them studies: offer a one-line time-skip when the player is waiting on lamps; compress changing gold; press the ban once so that refusing costs something; one warmth from the coin when a hook is hers; the cat reports smells and not conclusions. The second answers already show the temptation landing on the player. You do not need more notes to prove that. You need one press that makes the refusal expensive.
>
> **Do not restart** the villain study or the frozen-world study this week. Those died on a two-hour process kill and a game-master turn conflict. Another fresh campaign will not tell you why. Fix the process limits first, or leave them.
>
> Leave about a third of the $60 unspent. A confirmation, two revised Table A sessions, one combat, one DeepSeek probe, and eight short promised endings should fit in the rest if the smoke test passes. If it does not, the unspent money is the correct result.

**Answer 2** (to T2b; 408 words)

> Stop the study. The reports answered the questions, so the $60 is no longer a research budget. Keep it unless a line below is the only way to finish something you already owe.
>
> The one thing the reports do not close is the word you gave on 25 October. None of the thirty were continued, and none were ended by the player. Science being done means they will not be continued later. A recap for a next session is no longer an ending. They are owed a close, not another sitting.
>
> Write those closes outside the harness. One short beat each: resolve the hanging roll if there was one, show what the last action did, and stop on a scene edge. Do not open a second session, do not re-roll the campaign, and do not send them back through the pending-card rule that cut eight of them off. The eight stuck sessions come first — turns 1, 3, 4, 11, 12, and 14 died on a button, not on a story. The twenty-two that hit the cap are the same debt in a milder form: they stopped mid-walk. A few lines each is the promise. A fresh twenty-turn run is a new study, and you are done with those.
>
> If you can write the thirty beats yourself, spend nothing. If a model drafts them, cap that well under the $60, one pass, no play loop. What is left stays with you.
>
> Do not patch the app for a matrix that will not run again. Wrong skill totals, swallowed “?” notes, sheet against records, leaked tags, and the long out-of-game audits are already in the reports. Fix them only if someone is actually going to play. The promised endings do not need that fix if they are written rather than played.
>
> Do not buy the leftover measurements. Two revised Table A sessions, a Coast Way fight, a DeepSeek probe, a villain replay, a frozen-world retry — those were instruments. The questions they were for are in the reports. Another fresh campaign will not improve a finished paper, and the process kills that ate the later studies are not worth paying to rediscover.
>
> Nothing else this week. No new tables, no prompt experiments, no “one more session to be sure.” The fiction already worked. The plumbing already failed in the same way thirty times. Write the endings, keep the money, and leave the app alone unless you mean to run it for someone.
